| M1.59 | **`robots.txt` failed open on every response that was not a 200, so a 503, a 429 and a connection timeout each granted the same unrestricted permission a 404 does.** `Response.ok` is `status == 200 and body is not None`, and both consumption sites read `robots.parse(resp.text() if resp.ok else None)` — where `parse(None)` returns a policy that allows everything. The rule §5.2 states for one case (*"a 404 on robots.txt is the common case for small shops"*) was implemented as though it covered all of them, which is **M1.12's shape one level up**: a correct sentence about a narrow case, generalised by the code that implemented it. RFC 9309 §2.3.1 separates three cases the code merged into two. **The crawl-delay is the worse half.** `RobotsPolicy.crawl_delay()` returns None whenever there is no parser, so a 503 or a 429 dropped the host's requested pacing *along with* its rules and the tool fell back to a default it chose for itself — crawling a server that was failing, or explicitly asking us to back off, at our own rate. §5.2 already refuses a domain outright when `crawl_delay` is too high (`crawl_delay_too_high`), so the project treated that number as load-bearing in one direction and discarded it in silence in the other. **The trigger condition is in the corpus.** The request log for run 29 records `snocks.com` returning **429 to six requests** — `/imprint`, `/legal`, `/rechtliches`, `/blogs/lifestyle` and two product pages — and `ekomia.de` returning **500 and two more 429s** (M1.45's storm, seen from the log side). Robots requests over the three logged runs are 46 × 200 and 15 × 301 with no 5xx and no 429, so the branch never fired; but a host rate-limiting our pages is exactly the host whose `robots.txt` 429s next, and under H1 that response granted permission to crawl everything at our own pacing on a server that had just said stop eight times. Only the ordering spared it. | **The tri-state, in one classifier that is a pure function of status and body** (`robots.for_response`), so it cannot drift from the error strings `net.get` happens to write (M1.42). **2xx** → the rules. **4xx** → unrestricted; that branch and the reasoning in its docstring are *kept*, because they are right about the case they are about. **5xx, 429, transport failure, redirect loop** → `unavailable`: **disallow all for this run**, recorded as a `robots_unavailable` note carrying the status or the transport error, so an operator can see why a domain produced nothing. A **refused redirect** stays unrestricted — it is M1.18's moved-domain shape (`doonails.de`, `germanelectronic.de`, 2 of 13), where the host actually fetched has its own `robots.txt` read before its first request, and RFC 9309 §2.3.1.1 says so too. **Not an exclusion**: `company.excluded` is a standing verdict about a lead and a 503 is a fact about one afternoon, so nothing is written to `company`. Persistence routes to `fetch_persistently_failing` at A7b's N — **3 runs on 3 distinct days** (M1.34, migration 009) — and never on a single occurrence. **The crawl-delay loss is now structurally impossible rather than fixed**: an `unavailable` policy allows nothing, so no request survives for a delay to pace, and `TestUnavailablePolicy` pins the pair together. | §5.2 |
| M1.60 | **A `robots.txt` that was retrieved and would not parse also fell open** — `parse` caught the exception and returned the same unrestricted policy an absent file returns. Same class as M1.59, lower exposure: stdlib `RobotFileParser.parse` **skips** lines it cannot read rather than raising, which is measured rather than assumed (`test_malformed_robots_does_not_abort` feeds it `\x00\x01 not robots at all` and it parses to no rules). | **`unavailable`, not unrestricted — the unreachable side of the tri-state**, and the reasoning is recorded because either side was arguable. It is **not** the 4xx case: the server has a file and served it, so "no rules stated" is contradicted by the bytes in hand, and the honest reading is *rules stated, not understood*. The errors are asymmetric in M1.4's sense — falling open crawls pages the shop may have forbidden, falling closed costs one company one run and says so in a note — and because the branch is close to unreachable, the safe side is very nearly free. | §5.2 |
| M1.61 | **M1.44's method selected the wrong `robots.txt` on one company, and the count survived anyway.** M1.44 tested each stored URL against *"the newest stored robots.txt for that company"*. Measured over the corpus: on `zecplus.de` the newest robots artifact is **`blog.zecplus.de`'s** (id 458, `Disallow:` — fully permissive), so all **31** of that shop's stored bodies were tested against a permissive file from a **different origin**, while `www.zecplus.de`'s own 3,624-byte file (id 1) went unread. Per-company-newest is the wrong key: §5.2 says twice that robots is keyed to the **origin**. Behind it, a second fact with wider reach — **two origins serving byte-identical `robots.txt` collapse into one `artifact` row.** `uq_artifact_identity` is `(company_id, kind, content_hash)`, so the row names whichever origin was recorded first: the request log shows `www.smoke2u.de/robots.txt` and `www.propellerdiscount.de/robots.txt` were each fetched **200, three times**, and **no artifact row names either** — 26 stored bodies sit on authorities the artifact table appears to have no policy for, when in fact it read one and filed it under a sibling. | **Measured, not repaired — the repair is M5's and this changes its shape.** M1.44's *conclusion* is confirmed four ways: **0 of 13** companies' newest robots artifact is anything but a 200-with-body (so H1's undercount hazard did not materialise on this corpus), and the disallow count is **exactly two** — artifacts 171 and 186 — under per-company-newest, under newest-200-with-body, under strict authority matching (which finds 171 and calls 186 *undecidable*, the apex file having been applied to a `www.` URL), and under authority-matching with a `www.`/apex sibling fallback (2, 0 undecidable). **M1.44's repair (a) must not be implemented as written**: "the newest stored `robots.txt`" reproduces the zecplus vacuity, and an origin-keyed lookup must treat a collapsed row as *the policy of every origin that served those bytes* — or, where that cannot be established, report **not verifiable** rather than *allowed*. Measurement in `docs/unit4-robots-tristate-findings.md`. | §4, §5.2, §5.5b |
| M1.62 | **`audit-politeness` was blind to M1.59 by construction, and would have passed over it.** The external review said the audit "won't catch it because the requests were correctly spaced". The sharper statement is the one that matters: if the policy was unrestricted, the audit measures the run against the **default** interval — the interval an unread policy leaves in place — and reports HELD. The instrument built to verify politeness could not see the one failure that makes politeness wrong, because that failure changes **which pages may be fetched**, not how fast they arrive. | **The audit reads the artifact table too, and fails on it**, following M1.19's instinct that a rule should be enforceable rather than stated. Two classes, reported and failing **separately**, because collapsing them would make the check cry wolf and a check nobody trusts is worse than none: **unavailable** (5xx, 429, no status) **fails**, and fails as a *breach* when bodies are stored for that company — pages fetched under a policy never read; **no file** (4xx, refused redirect) is **reported and does not fail**, per RFC 9309 §2.3.1.2 and because the only instance of the second in this corpus is M1.18's moved domain. The database is now **required**: an audit run without one prints `NOT CHECKED` rather than looking green. It reads stored *state*, not a run — `artifact` carries no `run_id` and failure rows update in place — which is a narrower claim than the one asked for, and is the one the table can support. On the corpus at `5f56560`: **0 unread, 2 stating no file, §5.2 HELD.** | §5.2 |

| M1.63 | **A lead moved a band overnight on the clock alone, and it was found by re-running `score` after a change that touches neither scoring nor the corpus.** Unit 4's mandated re-run moved `navucko.com` **17 (D) → 42 (C)**. Nothing was crawled, no signal changed, and runs computed with the working tree and with `5f56560`'s ruleset are byte-identical — so the movement is not the code's. It is `neg.active_content` (**−25**, §6.3's largest) ceasing to fire between 2026-08-16 and 2026-08-17: the shop's dated posts are `2025-12-01, 2026-01-06, 2026-02-17, 2026-04-11, 2026-06-07, 2026-06-20`, and `(2026-08-17 − 2026-02-17).days == 181`. One post crossed `SIX_MONTHS = 180` overnight, `recent` fell **4 → 3**, and the rule's `>= 4` threshold is on the other side of that boundary. **Two hard thresholds compounding, with a 25-point step between them and a band boundary underneath.** The rule is doing exactly what §6.3 says; what had never been observed is how little has to change for the largest number in the ruleset to switch. | **Recorded, not fixed. No threshold is touched** — §6.5's bands and §6.3's weights are the spec's, and §10.3 already forbids calibrating them on this corpus. Two things follow instead. **(a)** §5.4's "score is a free recompute" is true of *cost* and not of *result*: a recompute is a function of `(signals, today)`, and the second argument moves on its own. Any claim of the form "the score did not change" must therefore name the date it was computed on, and a diff between two score runs on different days is not evidence about the code. **(b)** Direction of error: this instance moves the score **up** (a penalty withdrawn), which is the direction §6.4 treats as expensive — an over-scored lead is not mis-ranked, it is called. It raised no flag, because with `blog_post_count = 6` and 6 dated posts the enumeration is complete and the rule *declines* rather than abstaining, so A7's guard is not engaged and nothing blocks contact. Whether a −25 that can be withdrawn by one day's clock advance should decay rather than step is a §6.3 question and is **open**, filed in §10.5. | §5.4, §6.3, §6.5, §10.3 |

| M1.64 | **Three tests depended on a resolver behaviour RFC 6761 makes optional, and passed on every machine the project was developed on.** `TestApexToWwwWithinTheSeededSite` (×2) and `TestRedirectsAreRateLimited::test_apex_and_www_hops_to_one_server_share_one_budget` built their URLs from `localhost` and `www.localhost`. §6.3 of RFC 6761 requires `localhost` to resolve and leaves **subdomains** to the implementation: macOS and systemd-resolved answer, most container images do not. Reproduced rather than assumed — a `socket.getaddrinfo` shim that fails `*.localhost` was applied to a worktree at `6a5e266`, and exactly those three failed with `AssertionError: None != 200`. **A fourth test was passing vacuously in that condition**: `test_an_apex_to_www_redirect_is_not_a_move` asserts `site_domain IS NULL` and no `domain_moved`, and a redirect whose target never resolves satisfies both — an absence assertion met by the request failing. | **The suite resolves its own hostnames, and the names are chosen so the shim cannot be dead weight.** `tests/fixture_server.resolves_to_loopback` maps names to 127.0.0.1 at `socket.getaddrinfo` — **the seam was verified, not assumed**: `net.Fetcher` is httpx over httpcore's sync backend, whose `connect_tcp` calls `socket.create_connection`, which looks `getaddrinfo` up as a module global; traced under httpx 0.28.1 / httpcore 1.0.9 by wrapping both and printing the stack. Scoped by context manager with LIFO restore, never a bare monkeypatch. **The names are `shop.invalid` / `www.shop.invalid`, not `.localhost`** — RFC 2606 §2 guarantees `.invalid` is never delegated, so the shim is load-bearing on *every* machine and deleting it fails the suite on the maintainer's laptop as well as in CI. Built on `.localhost` the tests would go back to passing by accident wherever the resolver was generous, which is how this survived to be found by an external reviewer. **A `@skipUnless` was refused**: a skipped politeness test is worse than a failing one, because it goes quiet on exactly the machines where it would run most. **A `Host:`-header fixture was refused too, and the reason was checked in the code rather than taken on the review's word**: `net.Fetcher.get` calls `limiter.wait(host_of(current))` where `current` is the URL, and `host_of` is `authority_of(url).removeprefix("www.")` — derived from the URL and never from a header, so both cases would key on `127.0.0.1:PORT` and the test could no longer tell apex from www. It would pass while measuring nothing, which is the defect it exists to catch. | §5.2, and `tests/fixture_server.py` |
| M1.65 | **8,255 lines of tests, a ruff config, and nothing that ran either.** Every finding in the 17 Aug external review was one the reviewer had to know to run — including M1.64, which had been failing in container environments for the life of the project. This is the project's own standard not applied to itself: M1.19 made `audit-politeness` exit non-zero precisely so politeness is *enforced* rather than stated, and an unenforced suite is an assertion by the same argument. | **`.github/workflows/ci.yml`**, three jobs, `permissions: contents: read`. **lint** — `ruff check .` and `ruff format --check .`, both, since the project treats them as separate gates. **test** — pytest on the `pyproject.toml` floor (3.11) and the development version (3.12); 3.13 is deliberately absent until an interpreter above 3.12 has actually run this suite, because an unverified version in a first CI is how a green light gets ignored the week it goes red. The suite runs under `env -u ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` — *removed*, not blanked — and a preceding step **fails the build** if either `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` or `PORTAL_LIVE_SMOKE` is present, so no job can reach a paid endpoint or the one live host, whoever adds a secret later. That is what turns Unit 2's injected-client seam from a claim into a measurement, and it closes **before** M5 rather than after. **The migration runner from empty to head is not a fourth job**: `test_migrate.py::TestApplyPending` already applies every migration to a fresh database, checks `user_version` against the highest on disk, and checks that a second run is a no-op — a separate job would re-run that path with weaker assertions. **politeness** — `data/` is gitignored and absent, so `tests/fixture_corpus` builds one from a loopback fixture server at the real 1 req/s floor (anything faster fails its own spacing audit), and the audit runs **twice**: green on a healthy corpus, and required to **exit non-zero** on the same corpus with `robots.txt` returning 503. A gate that can only go green is a green light wired to nothing, and M1.62 is exactly the case where spacing still measures fine. **Run, not just written**: workflow run `32046900494` on this unit's push is green on all four jobs — including Python 3.11, the floor, which no interpreter on the development machine could check — and the audit job's log shows `§5.2: HELD` on the healthy corpus and `§5.2: BREACHED` on the 503 one. **And it catches the bug it shipped alongside**: the same pytest command run against `6a5e266` under a resolver answering for no `.localhost` subdomain gives `3 failed, 528 passed`. | §5.2, §7, §3 |
| M1.66 | **`score` records when a row was written, not the date it was scored against — and M1.63 made that the difference between reproducible and not.** M1.63 established that `evaluate` is a function of `(signals, today)` and that a band therefore has no meaning without its evaluation date. The `score` table's only temporal column is `computed_at TEXT NOT NULL`, written from `utc_now()` inside `_persist`, while `today` comes from `ScoreStage.today` — **two expressions for what is assumed to be one fact**, which is M1.42's shape. They agree in production only because nothing injects `today` outside tests, and nothing enforces that. Measured, and the gap is not subtle: a stage constructed with `today=date(2020, 3, 7)` writes a row reading `computed_at = 2026-08-17T16:32:15Z`, and **2020-03-07 is recorded nowhere at all**. A run straddling midnight UTC produces the same divergence with no injection involved. | **Not built here — it is an M5 prerequisite and it precedes any spend against a band.** The fix is a column recording the date `evaluate` was actually given, written from the same expression that feeds the rules rather than from a second clock read, so the two cannot name different days. Registered in §10.4b alongside the other two M5 preconditions. Filed rather than fixed because a schema change belongs with the milestone that has a migration in it, and because Unit 5's scope is M4 and M2. | §4, §5.4, §6.5 |
| M1.67 | **`FetchStage.run_company` was 310 lines holding four closures over three mutable dicts, and the address guard (H2) had nowhere to land in it.** `policies`, `unfetchable`, `policy_for`, `hop_allowed` and `allowed` were locals of the longest method in the project, so *which robots.txt governs this URL* had no name, no type, and no test that could reach it without running a whole company. The external review of 17 Aug asked for the extraction as **L1 — a readability item**; the reason it is sequenced **before** H2 rather than after is not readability. | **Extracted to `portal/sitepolicies.SitePolicies`, as a pure no-behaviour-change commit, and the no-change claim is measured rather than asserted.** The suite is unchanged at **537 passed, 2 skipped, 98 subtests**, and beyond that the fixture corpus was built from the same fixtures at `d57ea64` and at the extraction and the two databases compared field by field: every `artifact` URL, status, error and byte count, every `signal` key, value and `evidence_url`, every `company.excluded_reason`, every `review_flag`, and every line of the request log are **identical**. The only differing fields are the `content_hash` and `bytes` of the four gzipped sitemap shards — and **the same commit run twice differs in the same fields and more of them** (4 `bytes` differences against the extraction's 2), because the fixture embeds its ephemeral port in the XML it serves. The measured difference is strictly inside the instrument's own noise floor. **Seeding stays with the caller**, deliberately: an unreadable seeded `robots.txt` stops the run (M1.59) and a seeded `Crawl-delay` over the cap **excludes the company**, and an exclusion is a standing verdict about a lead that a per-authority memo has no business taking. `SitePolicies` is **one instance per company run** and never shared — a `robots.txt` is a statement about one afternoon, so a memo that outlived the run would let one shop's 503 silence another's. | §5.2, §3 |
| M1.68 | **A redirect could send the crawler into the operator's own machine, and the page it found was stored as the shop's.** `net.Fetcher` walks redirect chains itself and the only question it asked of a hop target was *does `robots.txt` permit it?* — a question **the target's own server answers**. Against a service on `127.0.0.1` that has no `robots.txt`, the probe 404s, RFC 9309 §2.3.1.2 reads that as *no rules stated*, and the hop is permitted. **Measured on `d57ea64`, not argued**: a fixture shop redirecting `/` to the portal's own §9 page — the default `portal serve`, unauthenticated, on the operator's database — fetched **9,537 bytes** of it and wrote them to `artifact` as that company's `homepage`, body naming a real prospect. So it is **two defects**: an exfiltration route out of a private network, and a **corpus-integrity** defect, since every later stage reads `artifact` by kind and would have read the operator's own UI as a German shop's front page. `169.254.169.254` is the same hole with credentials behind it. | **A second gate, on the address rather than the path (`portal/addresses.py`), applied in the transport to every URL and not only to hops** — a seed row can name an address exactly as a `Location` header can, and net.py's standing argument is that a hard requirement lives below the callers because there is no other way to issue a request. Six decisions, each of which could have gone the lazy way: **(a)** the refused networks are **written down** rather than delegated to `ipaddress.is_private`, whose membership has changed between CPython releases (100.64.0.0/10 among them) and CI runs 3.11 **and** 3.12 — a version-dependent guard is a guard whose behaviour nobody can state; **(b)** an IP literal is judged directly and **never resolved**, so the metadata case cannot be defeated by anything DNS does; **(c)** an IPv4-mapped v6 address is unwrapped and judged as its IPv4, or `::ffff:127.0.0.1` reads as public; **(d)** **every** resolved address is judged and one bad answer refuses the name — a resolver returning one public and one loopback address is the shape of a rebinding attack, and picking the reassuring half is how a guard is talked out of firing; **(e)** an unresolvable name is `address_unverifiable` and **refused**, which is M1.59's ruling one layer down — *not verifiable* rather than *allowed*, recorded distinctly from a refusal because "this pointed somewhere it must not" and "DNS was down" send an operator to different places; **(f)** the resolver is looked up **at call time, not import time**, because `resolves_to_loopback` rebinds the `socket.getaddrinfo` attribute (M1.64) and a guard that asks a different resolver than the client is about to ask is not a guard but a second opinion about a different question. That one is a test, not a comment: bound early the reason reads *did not resolve*, looked up late it reads *loopback*. **The seam is `AddressPolicy.loopback_permitted()`**, in `HostRateLimiter.unthrottled`'s idiom — the whole suite fetches from loopback, so it must exist; it widens **loopback alone**, so a test running on 127.0.0.1 still refuses `169.254.169.254`, and the guard is live in the suite rather than switched off by it. **Direction of error:** over-blocking costs one URL and records why in `artifact.error`; under-blocking fetches and *stores* an internal page under a company. **Anti-vacuity, measured:** with the check disabled, the 5 new transport-level tests go red and **all 537 pre-existing tests still pass** — nothing in 8,255 lines of tests could see this, which is M1.65's finding one level in. | §5.2, §3, §9 |
| M1.69 | **§10.4b told the ledger query to sum two tables that already contain each other.** It specifies the query over `run.est_cost_usd` **and** `llm_batch.est_cost_usd`/`actual_cost_usd`, while §7 control 4 says a batch reserves into **both** `llm_batch.est_cost_usd` *and* `run.est_cost_usd`. Read together, every batch is counted twice, and §7 control 2's own draft query — which sums `run` alone — contradicts §10.4b outright. Settled from the schema rather than from either prose: `llm_batch.run_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES run(id)` gives every batch exactly one submitting run, which is also what makes B3.1 coherent, so the `run` row is the ledger and the `llm_batch` row is the per-batch record of a line already in it. | **`run` is summed alone (`portal/ledger.monthly_spend_usd`), and §7 control 2 now says why.** The double-count fails **closed**, which is the safe direction and is why this is a defect rather than an incident — but the effective ceiling would have been **$22.50 against a stated $45** on all-batch spend, and a guard that trips at half its stated value teaches its operator to raise it without reading it. That is **M1.23's exact failure one level down**, and M1.23 is the finding that raised the ceiling to $45 precisely so it would stop tripping on correct behaviour. Tested at the seam: a batch reserved into both tables is counted **once**. | §7 control 2, §10.4b |
| M1.70 | **§10.4b said the schema defines "no month boundary and no query"; §7 control 2 defines both, and had since v0.3.** Control 2 states a **rolling 30-day** window and carries the literal SQL `SUM(est_cost_usd) FROM run WHERE started_at > datetime('now','-30 days')`. Both readings stood in the same document, so the two sections contradicted each other, and the §10.4b reading is the one that invites an implementer to pick a fresh window — re-deciding a question §7 had already answered, which is the failure mode §10.4b was written to prevent. It is the second instance of the class: M1.67's sequencing bug was a review heading contradicting its own body, and this is a spec section contradicting another spec section. | **§7 control 2 is kept intact and §10.4b is corrected to point at it.** No new window was chosen and none needed choosing. The boundary is keyed on **`run.started_at`** and on nothing else, which is what keeps it consistent with **B3.1**: a batch submitted in one window and reconciled in the next reconciles against its **submitting** run, whose `started_at` does not move, so no money crosses a window boundary at reconciliation time. The consequence §7 now states rather than leaves implicit: spend **ages out** 30 days after its run started, reconciled or not — which is what "rolling" means and is correct for a runaway guard, though it would be wrong for an accounting record. | §7 control 2, §10.4b |
| M1.71 | **`assert_prices()` is named twice in this spec and was never written.** §7 control 10 and M1.52's own resolution column both say `portal/llm.py` carries `assert_prices()` at import. `grep -rn assert_prices portal/` returns **nothing**; the file has a single `assert_declared` ([`llm.py:205`](../portal/llm.py), called at import on line 659) covering `PRICES` and `LIMITS` together. The spec has been describing a function that does not exist for two units, and the Unit 7 brief inherited the error and asked for the new assertion to be modelled on "`assert_prices` beside it". | **Both spec references corrected to `assert_declared`**, and the reason the tables share one assertion written down: a priced model with **no declared limits** is callable at an assumed parameter surface, which is M1.50's whole finding, so splitting the assertion in two would have hidden the check that catches it. Recorded rather than fixed silently because *"a function the spec describes and the code does not have"* is the same defect class as B7 in §10.4 — a table row that reads as implemented. | §7 control 10, M1.52 |
| M1.72 | **§7 control 4 requires two writes and does not say they are one transaction, and control 2 reads only one of them.** Control 4 reserves into `llm_batch.est_cost_usd` and `run.est_cost_usd`; control 2's ledger sums `run` alone (M1.69). A crash between the two writes therefore leaves the batch on the books and the **ledger blind to it** — an under-count. Every other failure mode in §7 is deliberately biased towards aborting: control 3's crash window "can only over-count, never under-count", and M1.52 refuses a fallback estimate outright. This is the one path in the section that fails **open**, and it was created by the interaction of two controls that are individually correct. | **Written into control 4 as a requirement: the two writes commit together.** Not built here — it needs the reservation caller, which is M5, and Unit 7's fence is control 2 plus the assertion. Named as an M5 obligation so the single-table query it depends on cannot be quietly invalidated by the code that arrives later. | §7 control 4 |
| M1.73 | **The instrument built to make a number rise visibly produced three incompatible numbers in four units — and the reason is this project's own standing defect, in its own instrumentation.** The line tracking the untransmitted audit section reported: **Unit 4** — no number (*"Carried forward"*); **Unit 5** — *"Five units have now reported around it"*; **Unit 6** — *"Five units have now reported around it"*, **byte-identical to Unit 5 and not incremented**; **Unit 7** — *"THREE units have now passed it over: Unit 5, Unit 6 and Unit 7"*, recounted on a **different definition** and with **Unit 4 omitted from its own membership**. Every reading after Unit 4 was taken from the previous unit's report rather than re-derived from the evidence, which is **M1.42's shape** (a second expression describing what the first one does) and **M1.44's** (a value carried forward rather than derived) — occurring in the apparatus this project uses to measure itself. Two independent causes, and both had to be fixed: a **scalar** that can only be copied, and **two definitions** — *"reported around it"* (Unit 5, which counted every unit in the project, giving five) versus *"passed it over"* (Unit 7, which counted units since Unit 5, giving three). **A defect in the measuring apparatus is worth more than the thing it was measuring**, which is why this is a numbered finding and not an erratum. | **The scalar is replaced by a named list with its derivation and its extension rule beside it**, in §Unit 2a's amendment above — the one place the count is now defined. The definition is settled as **"units that passed it over"**, meaning *a unit whose findings document records the section as still outstanding*, chosen because it has exactly one mechanical test (`grep -rlniE "LLM-generated ?/ ?hallucination" docs/unit*-findings.md`, which matches the section's **title** so that M5's report is not enrolled by discussing the subject while closing it) while *"reported around it"* is ambiguous about whether the discovering unit counts — which is precisely how Unit 5 reached five. Membership is **Units 4, 5, 6 and 7 — four**; Unit 2a is excluded because it *found* the absence rather than deferring it, and it has no findings document, so the grep excludes it without anyone having to decide. **Units 4–6's reports are left uncorrected**: they are the evidence, and rewriting them would destroy the record the finding rests on. Unit 7's line is redirected here, because it is the one the next unit would have copied. **No migration, no test, no CI surface** — the fix is that the next unit *derives* instead of *remembering*. | §Unit 2a amendment, and every future `## Still open` |
| M1.74 | **M1.66 closed: `score` now records the date `evaluate` was given.** Reproduced before it was fixed, on `ff99e20`: a `ScoreStage` constructed with `today=date(2020, 3, 7)` persisted a row reading `computed_at = 2026-08-18T21:17:27Z`, with the columns `id, company_id, run_id, phase, total, band, ruleset_version, computed_at` and **2020-03-07 recorded nowhere** — six years apart, and the evaluation date unrecoverable from the row. `computed_at` is a clock read taken in `_persist`; `today` came from `ScoreStage.today`; **two expressions for what is assumed to be one fact** (M1.42's shape). They agreed in production only by accident, and a run straddling midnight UTC diverges with no injection at all. | **`score.evaluated_on` (migration 010), written from the value `evaluate` actually used.** The date is carried on `ScoreResult` and set inside `evaluate` from its own `today` parameter, so `_persist` writes the date the rules were run against rather than re-reading `self.today` — **the two cannot name different days because there is only one expression**, which is a stronger guarantee than writing `self.today` in both places. Not backfilled: an inferred date would be right almost always and wrong exactly at the midnight boundary the column exists for (see §4). **Migration 010's header records that the interrupted M5 stash also used the number 010 for a different file** (`llm_batch_request`, no writer), so a later reference to "migration 010" is not matched to the wrong thing. (That header called the file unrecoverable; it is not — see M1.98. The number is still taken, so nothing about this migration changes.) `score_component` needs no column: `_persist` rewrites components wholesale with their parent, verified in the code. | §4, §5.4, §6.5, §10.4b |
| M1.75 | **M1.61 closed: the robots lookup is keyed on the origin that served the file, and reports *not verifiable* where it cannot be established.** Re-measured on the 17-August corpus at `641ff23`, and M1.61's account holds with one correction and one mechanism it did not name. **zecplus.de**: two robots rows, id 1 `https://www.zecplus.de/robots.txt` (3,624 bytes) and id 458 `https://blog.zecplus.de/robots.txt` (173 bytes, `Disallow:` — fully permissive). `_ROBOTS_SQL`'s `ORDER BY id DESC LIMIT 1` returns **458**, so the shop's stored bodies were tested against a permissive file from a different origin while its own went unread. The count is **29 bodies, not 31** — 27 on `www.zecplus.de`, 2 on `blog.zecplus.de`; 31 is the company's total `artifact` row count, which includes the two robots rows. **smoke2u.de and propellerdiscount.de**: 26 bodies (11 and 15) on `www.` hosts whose authority names no robots row — and the mechanism is not a missing fetch. The request log records **both** apex and `www.` robots.txt fetched 200 three times each; both origins served byte-identical files, `uq_artifact_identity` `(company_id, kind, content_hash)` collapsed them to one row, and `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` sets only `last_checked_at` and `http_status` — **not `url`** — so the surviving row keeps the *first* origin written and the second origin's fetch leaves no trace. A collapse is invisible in the table by construction: a corpus-wide query for robots rows sharing a hash within a company returns **0 groups**, because after a collapse there is only ever one row. | **The lookup is origin-keyed; the identity index is untouched.** `authority_of` — already the project's single expression for *whose robots.txt governs this URL*, and already used this way at `fetch.py:181` — selects the robots row whose authority matches the body's. Where no such row exists the consumers now build `robots.unavailable(...)`, which `allows()` answers **False** to, rather than the permissive `None` they built before. The tri-state M1.59 built is what makes this a lookup change and not a schema change: **`unavailable` (we cannot tell whose file this was) and `unrestricted` (the shop stated no rules) are already distinct objects with distinct reason strings**, and they send an operator to different places. **No migration was taken and `011` remains free for M5.** A stored `origin` column was weighed and rejected: it can only be backfilled as `authority_of(url)`, which is what the lookup computes, so it recovers nothing for rows already on disk and adds **a second expression for a fact `url` already determines** — M1.42's shape, and the `ON CONFLICT` column list above is exactly how such a pair drifts. Adding `origin` to `uq_artifact_identity` was rejected on the same measurement: it prevents *future* collapses but un-collapses nothing, because the absorbed row was never written — so it buys no recovery for the corpus M1.61 is about while rewriting the identity of every stored row. **What neither shape recovers, stated plainly: for a body on origin B whose company's only robots row names origin A, nothing on disk can establish what B served. The bytes were never stored under B, and only a re-fetch can answer it.** That is precisely the case the new state reports as not verifiable. **Direction of error: it over-reports.** A collapsed row that genuinely did govern both origins is now called not verifiable, which costs that company a run and says why; the alternative is bodies fetched under a policy nobody read, which is H1's whole family. | §4, §5.2, §7, §10.4b |

**One section of the audit was not transmitted and is not in the repository.** It is headed *"LLM-generated/hallucination signals"* and it is **missing, not empty** — the branch is byte-identical to `main` and carries no audit artifact, and there are no pull requests or issues on the remote to recover it from. On this project that heading most plausibly lands on §10.4: patterns that look plausible and were never observed. **Unit 2a is therefore complete but the audit is not closed**, and the conclusions above that would need revisiting if that section turns out to be substantive are named in the unit's report rather than assumed benign.

**THE COUNT OF UNITS THAT HAVE PASSED IT OVER IS DEFINED HERE AND NOWHERE ELSE (M1.73).** It was previously a scalar re-typed into each unit's report, and it produced three incompatible readings in four units. The canonical form is this list, and the rule for extending it:

> **Definition.** A unit has *passed it over* when its **findings document** records the section as still outstanding. Unit 2a is deliberately not a member: 2a is where the absence was **found and reported**, not deferred, and it has no findings document — so the definition excludes it mechanically rather than by anyone's judgement.
>
> **Derivation, not memory.** Re-run the grep. Never increment the previous unit's number, and never copy it:
>
> ```
> $ grep -rlniE "LLM-generated ?/ ?hallucination" docs/unit*-findings.md | sort
> docs/unit4-robots-tristate-findings.md
> docs/unit5-portability-and-ci-findings.md
> docs/unit6-address-guard-findings.md
> docs/unit7-cost-ceiling-findings.md
> docs/unit8-m5-prerequisites-findings.md
> docs/unit9a-phase2-scaffolding-findings.md
> ```
>
> It matches the **section's title**, not the word *hallucination*, and that is deliberate: **M5's report will discuss hallucination signals substantively**, because that is what the missing section is about, and a looser grep would enrol it as having passed the section over while it was closing it. The optional space accommodates Unit 7's spelling (`LLM-generated / hallucination`) against Units 4–6's (`LLM-generated/hallucination`). Verified to return exactly one match in each of the four files and none in `unit0`.
>
> **Members, as of Unit 9a (2026-08-20): Unit 4, Unit 5, Unit 6, Unit 7, Unit 8, Unit 9a — six.** Each unit added itself by re-running the grep and confirming the existing membership first, which is what the rule requires. **Unit 9a is a member despite writing the section's content**, and that distinction is the one worth holding on to: §6 of its report reconstructs *what the section would have had to cover for this code* and says in its own first line that this does not close the audit. A reconstruction written by the implementer of the code under review is not an independent audit of it. The original text was never transmitted and is unrecoverable; the grep matches the **title**, and the title is still there because the item is still outstanding.
>
> **To extend:** add your unit to this list when your own findings document records the section as outstanding, having first re-run the grep above and confirmed the existing membership. A unit's `## Still open` section should **point here** rather than restate a number, because restating it is the mechanism that broke it.

**The earlier readings are left uncorrected in Units 4, 5 and 6's own reports, on purpose.** They are the evidence M1.73 is derived from, and rewriting them would destroy the record that makes the defect legible. Only Unit 7's line was amended, and only to redirect it here — it is the number the next unit would otherwise have copied.

**There is a second counter of the same design, and it has NOT drifted — recorded as an observation, not a fix.** M3's *"how many units have recorded the repository is still public"* is also a scalar re-typed into each report, and it is currently **correct at every step**: Unit 5 *"verified twice — Unit 4 and Unit 5"* `{4,5}` = 2, Unit 6 *"three units"* `{4,5,6}` = 3, Unit 7 *"fourth unit"* `{4,5,6,7}` = 4. So the defect class is *a scalar that can only be copied*, and the M3 instance has so far been copied correctly. It is named here so that the next divergence is **recognised rather than re-derived**, which is what §10.4 exists for — and if it does drift, the fix is the one above: replace the scalar with the list and the grep, do not adjudicate the number.

### Amendments from Unit 9a — the Phase-2 scaffolding — 2026-08-20

**M5 phase 9a. Fixtures only: no crawl, no API call, no spend, no `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` at any point.** Four of §7's open register rows turned out to be Phase-2 *blockers* rather than backlog — a signal cannot be written without a key, and a value cannot be scored without a confidence rule — so they are settled here, before the first Phase-2 write exists, rather than alongside it. **Every one was re-derived against the artefact that decides it rather than taken from the register** (M1.73's rule), and one of the four had a stale citation.

**Migrations taken: `011`, `012`, `013`. Next free: `014`.**

**M1.85 was written into this table on 2026-08-20 by Unit 9b, not by 9a.** 9a made the finding, cited it three times in its own report and named it in a test docstring, and never amended the register that owns it — so for the length of one merge the finding existed only in a unit report. That is **M1.71 inverted** — M1.71 was a spec line naming an `assert_prices()` that no code implemented; this is an implemented, tested finding that no spec line named — and it is **M1.76's shape exactly**, a ruling filed where the register that owns it cannot see it. Verified before it was fixed: `git grep "M1\.85" -- docs/ portal/` returned the 9a findings document and one test docstring, and nothing in this file. Recorded here rather than backdated silently, because the amendment table's date is what makes it auditable.

| # | Finding | Resolution | Sections |
|---|---|---|---|
| M1.76 | **A2 closed: the field→key mapping existed, and existed nowhere the code could reach.** It was ratified on 2026-08-16 — all ten items — inside `docs/a2-phase2-signal-mapping-proposal.md`, and the spec was never amended. A proposal with a `RULED` heading is not a specification: `extract-p2` cannot write a signal it has no key for, and the register's own summary of the gap (*"`HomepageExtract.own_brand` still has no view column"*) was the visible half of it. The invisible half is worth more: **`qual.own_brand` is a live +10 rule declared `reads=()` whose predicate returns `declines()` unconditionally — not because the answer is no, but because no key existed** — and `assert_declared` carried a **named exemption** for it, so the one check built to catch a rule that cannot fire had the instance written into it as a special case. It is `phase2_reachable`, so its +10 sits in every unbanked company's `remaining_upside`, and on the corpus that is exactly what admits `germanelectronic.de` to paid extraction: 5 + 50 = 55, the B floor to the point, and 45 without it. | **The ruling is transcribed into §5.5b with one column the proposal did not carry — the rule that reads each key** — because a field and its reader are only auditable side by side. Both audits were then run rather than asserted: **eleven fields have no reader** and every one is deliberate (§8's brief, §9's row, or §10.2's lever kept deliberately unscored); **no rule now lacks a field**, where before exactly one did. `brand.own_brand` and a `company_profile` column arrive in migration 011, `qual.own_brand` gains `reads=("own_brand", "homepage_extracted")`, and **`assert_declared`'s exemption is deleted** — the exemption existed only because the key did not. | §5.5b, §4, §6.1, §10.6 |
| M1.77 | **A3 closed: `agency.footer_credit` was about to acquire a second writer on a −20 rule with no merge rule stated.** `parsers.footer_agency_credit` writes it deterministically and `_has_agency` reads it; `HomepageExtract.agency_credit` is the second writer and arrives with M5. Left unstated, the winner is whichever stage ran last, and re-running the free `extract-p1` after a paid `reconcile` would silently revert a paid value to a regex one. | **There is no merge: two keys, one reader.** The LLM value goes to `agency.footer_credit_llm`, which **no §6 rule may read and no view column exposes** — `content.blog_lastmod_hint`'s treatment. The choice is structural rather than semantic **because §10.4's platform-vocabulary exclusion cannot be enforced in a prompt**: *"powered by JTL-Shop"* has already caused two opposite defects, the deterministic parser holds the exclusion as a testable regex (`parsers._PLATFORM_CREDIT`), and re-stating that list inside a system prompt is neither substring-verifiable nor testable without spending money. Withholding the reader **is** the guard. A test asserts that no `Rule.reads` entry and no view column names an unscored hint key, so a future writer that wires it in fails rather than ships a −20. **Direction: it under-detects, leaving the lead too high** — the same direction §6.3 already accepts for this rule, now with the second instrument's opinion *stored* so the two can be compared on a corpus later. | §5.5b, §6.3, §10.4 |
| M1.78 | **§5.5b left "what text is sent to the model" unstated and §10.2's base rate hung on it.** Recorded as an open M5 decision in two places: whichever of `parsers.visible_text` or raw HTML is sent decides whether the `Inh.`/`Inhaber` marker's base rate is 1/12 or 3/12. | **Cleaned visible text — and this is less a new decision than the one §5.5b's own input-preparation requirement already made without writing down.** *Strip `<script>`, `<style>`, `<svg>`, `<nav>`, comments; reduce to text + structural tags* **is** `visible_text`'s contract; sending raw HTML would contradict the paragraph two below it. Two consequences, both wanted. **(a)** The model sees what a human reading the page sees, which is the only footing on which "verified by substring presence in the cleaned page text" means anything — checking an extraction against text the model was never shown is M1.43's shape, and `portal/verify.py` takes the **sent text** as its argument so it structurally cannot reach for a different one. **(b)** §10.2's base rate is fixed at **1 of 11**; the two extra domains carry the token only inside a `<script>` block, which `visible_text` decomposes on purpose so JSON-LD vendor identifiers do not land in the provider block. §10.2 stays open — a larger corpus still settles it — but now by a measurement someone else could reproduce. **Rejected and recorded:** raw HTML raises the yield by feeding the model exactly the contamination §5.3 strips, so its extra hits are not evidence about the shop. | §5.5b, §10.2 |
| M1.79 | **A4 closed: §5.5b said an unverified value is *"discarded"*, and `discarded` had no implementation.** `company_profile` pivots latest-per-key with **no `confidence` predicate**, at any of its three revisions — the register cited `001_initial_schema.sql:168`, which is a **stale pointer**: the live view is migration 007's. The claim held at the new address. M5 is what makes `confidence ≠ 1` reachable at all, so today's view would have fed a value the tool measured and rejected straight into `evaluate`. Measured before the change: **2,404 signal rows, every one `method='deterministic'` with `confidence IS NULL`** — the filter is a no-op on the corpus, which is why this is the only moment it can be added without also repairing data. | **`confidence IS NULL OR confidence > 0` (migration 012).** NULL passes because §4 defines it as deterministic. `0` is excluded and **no threshold above 0 is invented** — a plausibility cut chosen on zero observations is M1.4's error, the one §10.3 refuses for *"fewer than N products is suspicious"*. **Placement is load-bearing and the obvious place is wrong:** the filter goes **after** `current_run`, never inside `observed`, or a Phase-2 run whose extractions all failed verification would stop being its stage's authoritative run and an older run's values would be served as current — M1.36 exactly, re-created by the guard meant to strengthen it. **Direction: too strict, and checkably so** — every §5.5b key that any §6 rule reads feeds a rule that *awards* (+15, +15, +15, +10), and the one Phase-2 key on a rule that subtracts is `agency.footer_credit_llm`, which M1.77 gives no reader. The filter therefore cannot withhold a penalty and cannot push a company toward a phone call. The loss is visible, not silent: the row stays in `signal`, §9 renders it red, and the two stage facts carry `confidence = 1` so they survive the filter and keep *rejected* distinguishable from *never ran*. | §4, §5.5b |
| M1.80 | **C4 decided: `uq_signal_identity` keeps `evidence_url`.** The concern is that `(run_id, company_id, key, evidence_url)` lets a redirect defeat dedup, and M5 is the first stage to write signals whose evidence is a URL that may have been redirected. | **It stays, on three things measured or derived before the decision rather than after.** (1) **The column has never disambiguated anything**: 2,404 signal rows, 2,404 distinct `(run_id, company_id, key)` triples, **zero** groups carrying two `evidence_url`s — dropping it today would change no row. (2) **`evidence_url` is not a live URL.** It is `artifact.url`, taken off the stored row in the same expression as `artifact_id` (M1.42), so a redirect is frozen into the artifact before any extraction runs and **the described case cannot arise in an extraction stage at all**, M5's included. §5.6's repeat-safe reconcile depends on the second invocation computing the same `evidence_url`, and it does, from a row that does not move. (3) **A duplicate would cost no score**: `company_profile` pivots `rn = 1` on a deterministic `(observed_at DESC, id DESC)` tiebreak, so one value surfaces and the same one every time; it costs §9 a second evidence link, and narrows D6's sentence to *"no duplicate observations **from the same document** within the same run"* — which is what the index always guaranteed. **Rejected on direction, not cost:** narrowing to `(run_id, company_id, key)` makes the second document's reading vanish through `DO NOTHING`, written nowhere and indistinguishable from never observed — **M1.75's collapse in a second table**. Over-recording is legible; under-recording is unrecoverable. **Unobserved and labelled unobserved:** no writer has ever produced a same-run two-document signal, so the tiebreak's behaviour there is untested against reality. | §4, §5, §5.6 |
| M1.81 | **`_own_brand` and `_owner_operated` had two states where the ruling needs three.** §6.1 settles what a failed span does; §5.5b explicitly instructs the model to return `null` for a field it cannot find, and nothing settled that. Three collapses were available and all three are wrong: `null → false` is *unverified read as absent* on a rule that awards points; `null →` decline is right until Phase 2 runs and wrong the moment it has, because a decline records no component and nobody is told; decline `→` abstain fills the queue with *"Phase 2 has not run yet"* for every company — §6.4's *"a queue that refills itself stops being read"*, manufactured. | **Three states, keyed on A2's stage fact.** Absent `llm.homepage_extracted` / `llm.impressum_extracted` → **decline** (`_ai_invisible` and `_slow_site`'s existing convention). Boolean written → **fire or decline**. Stage fact written, boolean absent → **abstain** with `own_brand_undetermined` / `owner_named_undetermined`. State 3 is reached two ways — the model returned `null`, or its span failed verification and M1.79's filter removed the row — and both take the same reason **deliberately**, because both send a person to the same page to answer the same question; `raised_note` carries which. `qual.owner_operated` abstains **only where disjuncts 1 and 2 have both declined**, so a company that already won its +15 raises no flag. **Direction: both err too low and block nothing** — A7a's own note, that the unverified *value* errs high while the *abstention* it produces errs low. **Migration 013 adds both reasons in the same commit as the code that raises them**, per M1.45(c). | §6.1, §6.4, §10.6 |
| M1.82 | **§5.4's gate can only loosen, and a company therefore keeps paying for answers it already has.** `remaining_upside` drops a rule only when Phase 1 **banked** it. Reproduced before anything was built, on §5.4's own worked example: a company whose Phase 2 has already run and answered *both* booleans **in the negative** still carries `upside=50` and is still `admitted=True`. Those 25 points are gone and nothing can award them; the bound does not know. **It cannot be derived from outcomes**, and the reason is A7's mechanism: `evaluate` records **no component at all** for a decline, so a Phase-2 `false` — the commonest outcome, and exactly the one that should tighten the bound — is invisible to any reading of `score_component`, and indistinguishable from *never evaluated*. | **`Rule.phase2_input_settled`, a declared per-rule callable** answering *has Phase 2 already answered this, for **this** company?* — distinct from `phase2_reachable`'s *could it, for anyone?*. It reads the stage facts, the only keys that separate *ran* from *never ran*. **`assert_declared` requires it on every Phase-2-reachable rule and refuses it on every other**, both directions enforced: a Phase-2 rule that forgets it silently inflates the bound, and a Phase-1 rule that grows one asserts something about a phase that cannot touch it. `evaluate` gains one term — a rule leaves the bound if banked **or** settled. **Direction: the gate can now only get tighter**, which is safe here for a reason no other tightening would have: the points are not unmeasured, they have been **measured and are not there**, so §5.4's *"not discarded without a human being told"* holds without needing the queue at all. Inert until a writer exists. *(`evaluate` already had a local named `settled` for ladder-chain tracking; it is renamed `chains_settled`, because two different meanings under one name in one function is how the next reader gets it wrong.)* | §5.4, §6.1 |
| M1.83 | **`extract-p2` had no entry point, so the ledger gate Unit 7 built had nothing to gate.** M1.71's `assert_ledger_guarded` fails the **import** on a paid callable nobody classified — a mechanism that has been correct and unexercised since Unit 7, because §7's controls 3 and 4 need a caller and the caller is M5's. | **The entry point lands with the client injected** (Unit 2's shape: `LLMProvider` is a Protocol, every test drives a fake), **classified in `PAID_SURFACES`, and decorated with `@requires_ledger_clearance`** — so it cannot run without a `LedgerClearance`, which only `ledger.check_ceiling` constructs. **It builds requests and spends nothing**: 9a stops at the point where §7 control 4's reservation would be made, because that reservation must make its two writes one transaction (M1.72) and that is 9b's. What 9a proves is that the gate *engages* — the negative control removes the classification and the **import** fails, which is the property the whole mechanism was built for. | §5.5, §7, §10.4b |
| M1.84 | **Two prose passages sat inside §4's `sql` code fences**, one added by M1.74's own amendment. Cosmetic in a renderer, not cosmetic in a grep: §4 is the section every schema question is answered from, and prose inside a fence is invisible to a reader skimming the SQL and mis-highlighted for everyone else. | **Both fences closed and reopened around the prose.** Recorded rather than fixed silently because it is the same class as B7 at the level of the document: a passage that *reads as* part of the schema when it is commentary on it. | §4 |
| M1.85 | **A4's *"the loss is visible"* argument has a dependency A4 did not state, and a negative control found it.** §9 renders a rejected value's red evidence **beneath its `score_component`**, and `evaluate` writes no component for a rule that *declines* (A7). So a `confidence = 0` row is renderable only where its rule still produces a component. Reproduced before it was reasoned about: `test_confidence_zero_is_red` gave a **deterministic** key (`platform.detected`) a synthetic `method='llm', confidence=0`, migration 012's filter removed the value, the rule declined, and the assertion went red on a behaviour change it was not about — the placeholder key was hiding the dependency. | **The guarantee holds, but THROUGH the abstention rather than independently of it, and that is the finding.** For every §5.5b key any §6 rule reads, M1.81's three-state predicates abstain where the extraction ran and the value was rejected, and **an abstention is a component worth 0 points** — so the component exists and the red evidence has somewhere to render. The two are therefore **one mechanism, not two independent guards**: remove the three-state predicates and A4's rejected rows become unrenderable, silently. The tests were moved off the placeholder onto `brand.own_brand`, a key that will really carry `confidence = 0`. **What a reviewer still cannot see is a rejected value on a key NO rule reads** — the nine §8/§9-only fields and `agency.footer_credit_llm` — because those produce no component on any path; the row stays queryable in `signal` and is invisible in §9. Bounded and recorded rather than closed: none of them is scored, so the invisibility costs no points, and closing it means a second §9 render path that does not hang off `score_component`. | §9, §5.5b, §10.6 |

### Amendments from Unit 9b — reconciliation — 2026-08-21

**M5 phase 9b. Fixtures only: no crawl, no API call, no spend, no `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` at any point.** `portal/reconcile.py` is the half of M5 that collects what `extract_p2` submitted, and it is the first code in this project that ever learns a **measured** cost rather than an estimate — which is why B3.2 and B3.3 close here and why §7 gains control 12.

**Migrations taken: `014`, `015`. Next free: `016`.** The 9a section above still reads *"Migrations taken: 011, 012, 013. Next free: 014"*, which was true when it was written and has been false since 9b's first commit; it is left standing as the record and superseded by this line.

**M1.86–M1.90 were written into this table on 2026-08-21 by Unit 9b-close, not by the commits that coined them.** All five were cited in production code, migrations and tests before any of them had a row here. That is the subject of **M1.91**, which is a finding about this table rather than about any of the five. Verified before it was fixed, mechanically: `git grep -oP 'M1\.\d+'` over `docs/ portal/ tests/` returned **90** distinct numbers; the amendment-table rows (`^| M1\.`) declared **85**; the difference was exactly M1.86, M1.87, M1.88, M1.89, M1.90.

| # | Finding | Resolution | Sections |
|---|---|---|---|
| M1.86 | **A batch can return fewer results than it was sent, and every guard built for M1.51 was blind to it.** §5.6 fact 2 is a rule about a **set** — *"a batch moves to `reconciled` only when EVERY ONE of its requests has a terminal disposition"* — and `llm.resolve_batch_status` read the **returned items** and nothing else, while `llm_batch` recorded only `request_count`, which is a number. A number cannot name *which* requests are still owed. Measured on the version that took only `items`: **ten requests sent, eight succeeded results returned → `resolve_batch_status` said `RECONCILED` and `resubmittable` said `()`**. Two companies silently unextracted, named nowhere, with the batch marked done and its reservation released. **This is M1.51 fact 2's own failure arriving through a different door**: M1.51 caught partial-ness arriving as an `expired` MEMBER, and this is partial-ness arriving as an **ABSENT** member, which no guard built for the first shape can see. The guard was correct and its input was incomplete. | **The request set is stored at reservation time (migration `015`, `llm_batch_request`) and the batch is resolved against the STORED set, never against the returned one.** `resolve_batch_status` gains a **required** keyword-only `expected` — required rather than defaulted, so that a caller which does not know what it sent has to say so at the call site instead of silently getting the weaker answer. A `custom_id` in `expected` with no returned item is a request with no terminal disposition: the batch stays `completed`, **no ledger correction is applied**, the full reservation stands, and the missing `custom_id`s are named in `BatchReport.still_owed` rather than counted. Two batch states declared in §4 and reachable from nothing (`expired`, `failed`) became reachable as a byproduct, since telling them from *still owed* is what needed the set. | §4, §5.6, §7 |
| M1.87 | **§5.5b's verification is defined against the text that was sent, and `reconcile` runs in a process that does not have it.** `verify.PageText` takes the sent text as an argument precisely so it cannot reach for a different rendering of the same page (M1.43). Across the submit/collect boundary — up to 24 hours, and results retrievable for 29 days (§5.6 fact 4) — that string is gone. Reconstructing it from the artifact is the only option that does not put 60 KB per company in the database, and it is sound as far as it goes: `custom_id` names the artifact, artifact bodies are content-addressed and immutable, and `extract_p2.clean` is one expression. **But *"sound as far as it goes"* is precisely the shape M1.43 was**, and the assumption has a real way to fail — `reconcile` may run under a **later build** than the one that submitted, and a change to `parsers.visible_text` between the two would have `verify` checking an extraction against a page the model was never shown, silently, with every test still passing. | **The reconstruction is turned from an assumption into a check.** `llm_batch_request.sent_text_sha256` stores the SHA-256 of the string that was actually sent, at reservation time; `reconcile` reconstructs, hashes, and compares. A mismatch is the disposition `text_unreproducible` and **no value is written at all** — not a value at `confidence = 0`, no value. **Direction: it errs low.** The extraction is lost and was paid for; the stage fact still records that the stage ran, and nothing unverifiable reaches a person. `text_unreproducible` is deliberately **not** a member of `llm.RequestOutcome`: `portal/llm.py` holds facts about a vendor, and this is this tool's opinion about its own ability to check a result, which is not the provider's business. | §4, §5.5b, §5.6 |
| M1.88 | **§5.5b named two fields for substring verification and A2's mapping showed the rest have destinations too.** The original sentence named `legal_name` and `managing_directors`/`owner_name`, because those are what goes in a letter. But `legal_form` is `qual.owner_operated` disjunct 1 at **+15**; `city` and `postal_code` are written to `company` and rendered in §9; the register fields are §8's brief. A field that reaches a person unverified is the failure this backstop exists for, whichever column it arrived in. | **Every text field of `ImpressumExtract` is substring-verified**, which is wider than §5.5b's original sentence and narrower than it sounds — it is the same instrument on more fields, not a stronger one, and M1.49's limit on what a span proves for a **boolean** is untouched. Recorded here because the widening is a change to a stated rule and would otherwise read as an implementation liberty. | §5.5b |
| M1.89 | **§7 control 4's own ordering creates a batch state the schema forbade.** Control 4 says *"reserve into both `llm_batch.est_cost_usd` and `run.est_cost_usd` **before the submit call returns**"*, and it must be taken literally, because the whole point is that a submitted batch is committed spend whether or not the process survives to read it. Taken literally, the reservation is written **before `messages.batches.create` returns** and therefore **before `provider_batch_id` exists** — and that column was `TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE`, so the reservation could not be written at all. **Both alternative orderings were weighed and both fail in the forbidden direction**: *(a)* submit, then write both rows in one transaction — a crash in the gap leaves money spent, the ledger blind, **and no `llm_batch` row at all**, so `reconcile` cannot even find the batch to collect the results already paid for; that is M1.72's defect made worse, and it under-counts control 2, the one direction §7 must not fail in. *(b)* write `run.est_cost_usd` first, submit, write `llm_batch` after — control 2 sums `run` alone so the ledger stays right, but a crash in the gap leaves spend attributable to nothing and a submitted batch with no local row, so its results are never collected: **spend with zero return, invisibly**, and it violates M1.72 by construction, which is the finding it would be implementing. | **A fourth batch status, `reserved` (migration `014`).** `provider_batch_id` becomes nullable, NULL **exactly** while `status = 'reserved'`, enforced by a CHECK rather than left to convention: `status = 'reserved' OR (provider_batch_id IS NOT NULL AND submitted_at IS NOT NULL)`. `submitted_at` becomes nullable with it, for the same reason — a batch that has not been submitted has no submission time, and writing the reservation's clock into that column would be a second meaning for one word; `reserved_at` is added and is `NOT NULL`. **A `reserved` batch is deliberately not in `OPEN_STATUSES`**: it has no provider id to poll, so it is reported to a human rather than retried, and **nothing releases its reservation automatically** (see M1.90c). | §4, §7 |
| M1.90 | **B3.2 and B3.3 closed: the ledger had never held a measured number, and nothing said what one may do when it arrives.** §7 controls 2–4 are built entirely on estimates — `run.est_cost_usd` is reserved before a call and `ledger.monthly_spend_usd` sums that column alone. B3.2 was open precisely because nothing had ever written an actual; B3.3 asked for the rule governing the write, and B3.1's ruling (*the correction goes to the submitting run*) settled only the **ownership** half of it. `reconcile` is the first code that ever learns an actual, and it was about to do so with the rule stated only in its own docstring — which is **M1.76's shape**, a ruling filed where the register that owns it cannot see it. | **§7 gains control 12, and it is four clauses each of which is a direction of error rather than a convenience.** *(a)* The correction goes to the **submitting** run (B3.1); the reconciling run gets its own `run` row for its own timestamps and does not absorb the delta. *(b)* It is applied **once**, and only at a terminal state; while a batch is still owed anything its reservation stands at full value, which **over-counts**, which is control 3's own stated preference. `reconciled_at` is written in the same transaction and makes *"once"* checkable. *(c)* **Nothing else ever releases a reservation** — not an expiry, not a failure, not a dry key, not a `reserved` batch whose provider id was lost; the only thing that corrects a reservation is a measured actual, and the release therefore **falls out of the arithmetic** (an expired request consumed nothing, contributes nothing to the measured total, and its share comes back with the delta). A rule that released spend on a *status* would be a rule that can be reasoned into releasing real money. *(d)* **The window does not move with it**: control 2 is keyed on `run.started_at` and nothing else (M1.70), so a batch submitted 40 days ago and reconciled today changes the current window's total by **zero**. Stated as an omission because it is deliberate: the actual is never *added* to the ceiling query — control 2 still sums `run` alone, and summing both would halve the effective ceiling, M1.69's double-count with actuals instead of estimates. | §5.6, §7 |
| M1.91 | **Two consecutive units shipped amendment numbers that exist nowhere in the register, and the rate is rising.** 9a coined **M1.85**, cited it three times in its own findings document and once in a test docstring, and never amended this table. 9b then coined **M1.86, M1.87, M1.88, M1.89, M1.90** and cited them **17 times across 7 files** — `portal/reconcile.py` (9), `portal/extract_p2.py` (2), `portal/migrations/015_batch_requests.sql` (2), `portal/cli.py` (1), `portal/llm.py` (1), `portal/llm_anthropic.py` (1), `portal/migrations/014_batch_reservation_states.sql` (1) — production code and schema, not reports. **This is M1.71 inverted, and it is M1.73's class**: M1.71 was a spec line naming an `assert_prices()` that no code implemented; this is implemented, tested, cited code naming rows the spec does not have. It is a defect **in the instrument this project uses to measure itself**, one level out from M1.73 — M1.73 was a miscounted number *inside* a report, and this is findings that never reached the register at all. Measured, not asserted: `git grep -oP 'M1\.\d+'` over `docs/ portal/ tests/` returned **90** distinct numbers against **85** declared rows. What a reader of `reconcile.py` could do on 2026-08-20: read a comment saying *"M1.86's whole finding"* and **not look up what M1.86 says**. | **A test, not a convention, because the precedents in this project are all tests** — `ruleset.assert_declared`, `assert_ledger_guarded`, M1.19's exit code. `tests/test_amendment_register.py` enumerates the tree with `git ls-files` (so caches and untracked scratch cannot influence it), greps every tracked file for `M1\.\d+`, parses the declared set from this table's row starts, and **fails naming every cited-but-undeclared number and the files that cite it**. It runs in the existing `test` job on both 3.11 and 3.12 and costs nothing. Two things it deliberately does **not** do: it does not require a declared row to be cited anywhere (a finding may be recorded and never referenced), and it does not check contiguity of the numbering, which is a different rule and has never been violated. | This table, and every future unit |
| M1.92 | **§7's controls have been numbered one way in the source and rendered another way since Unit 2, and 50 citations point through the gap.** The list ran `1, 2, 3, 4, 10, 11, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9` in the file. Markdown numbers an ordered list **by position**, so what a reader sees is `1…12` in file order — control **10** rendered as *5*, **11** as *6*, and 9b's new control 12 (inserted where 10 and 11 already sat) as *7*, while `5`–`9` rendered as *8*–*12*. **Eight of twelve controls rendered under a number other than the one written.** Measured across `docs/ portal/ tests/`: `control 2` ×74, `control 3` ×11, `control 4` ×55 are unaffected (positions 1–4 were correct); `control 6` ×2, `control 8` ×7, `control 9` ×4, `control 10` ×9, `control 11` ×19, `control 12` ×9 — **50 citations** — resolve to the wrong paragraph for anyone reading the rendered document. A reader following `llm_anthropic.py` to *"§7 control 11"* (the prepaid-balance rule) lands on *"Content-hash short-circuit"*. **The same class as M1.84 at the level of the document**: a passage that *reads as* one thing and *is* another, cosmetic in neither a renderer nor a grep. | **The three misplaced items are moved into position; no control text changed and no control renumbered.** Written order is now `1`–`12` and equals rendered order, verified by parsing the list and comparing written labels against positional index. Fixed rather than only recorded because it is a pure reordering of whole list items — small, local, and reversible — and because leaving it would mean shipping control 12 into a list that mis-renders it. The 50 citations become correct without any of them being edited. | §7 |
| M1.93 | **Spec line 113's open-findings list is a carried-forward scalar, and Unit 8 said so without being able to fix it.** *"Remaining findings (A1–A4, B1, B3.2–B3.3, B5–B7, C1–C4) are still open and are not required by M0 or M1"* was written at M0/M1 time and never re-derived. Unit 8 derived the register against the artefacts and found **four** members already closed underneath it — **A1** (closed by M1.21→M1.22 and ratified 2026-08-16, *in this same document*), **B6**, **B7**, **C3** (premise dissolved) — and recorded the correct fix: *"line 113 should be replaced by a pointer to §7's method rather than corrected in place, since correcting the membership leaves the mechanism that rots it"*, deferring it as a spec-structure change. 9b closes **B3.2** and **B3.3**, taking the count of stale members to **six of twelve** — the sentence is now wrong about half its own membership. | **The membership list is deleted and replaced by a pointer to the method.** Correcting it in place is what every unit since M1 has declined to do, and correctly: a frozen list of names is exactly M1.73's scalar, and the eighth unit to edit it would be the eighth to copy it. The authority is now the per-unit `## Open items register — derived, not remembered`, which is re-derived row by row against the artefact that decides each row. **Unit 8's recommendation is implemented as written, and credited rather than re-discovered.** | §1, and every future unit's findings document |
| M1.94 | **M1.91's check is more permissive before a commit than after one, and its first CI run failed on the document that describes it.** Two results in one failure. *(a)* **A document that quotes a citation is a citation**: `docs/unit9b-reconciliation-findings.md` recorded the negative control by reproducing the injected line, and the check refused it — correctly, by its own rule. *(b)* **The local run and the CI run were not the same check.** The scan enumerates with `git ls-files`, chosen so that `.mypy_cache` and untracked scratch could not influence a check about what the repository says; the unforeseen consequence is that an **untracked** file is invisible to it. Four consecutive local full-suite runs passed while the offending document sat untracked, and it went red the instant the file was committed and CI ran. Found by CI and by nothing else — and only because opening the PR was the first time any of Unit 9b had ever triggered the workflow, which fires on push-to-main and `pull_request` and had therefore never seen three pushed branch commits. | **Fixed in the document, not in the check.** The control's number is written `M1.<994>` where it is quoted, which does not match `M1\.\d+`, with the substitution stated at both quotation sites. **An exclusion was considered and rejected**: *ignore a citation on a line containing "negative control"* is a hole with a name, and a rule that can be opted out of by writing a phrase is the convention this test exists to replace. The permissive-before-commit property is **recorded rather than removed** — closing it would mean scanning the working tree, which is what `git ls-files` was chosen to avoid — and it points where M1.19 already points: the authority is the run that gates the merge, not the run on the author's machine. | This table, and §Unit 9b's findings |

### Amendments from Unit 9c-prep — the corpus, the convention, and the register — 2026-08-21

**No crawl, no API call, no key, no spend.** Unit 9c was briefed as the first real spend and stopped 89 seconds in, on two blockers it could verify and not clear: the 13-company corpus was not on the machine, and `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` was unset. This unit is the reconciliation of what that stop uncovered — one destroyed artefact, one false statement in this document that had propagated into three shipped migration headers, and two claims recorded as values that were never measured. **Migrations taken: none. Next free: `016`.**

| # | Question or defect | Resolution | Section |
|---|---|---|---|
| M1.95 | **A mitigation outlived the defect it was written for, was carried by six consecutive briefs, and destroyed the thing it was protecting.** Unit 7's first push failed both pytest jobs while the suite was green locally — `llm-prices --reserve` opened the database and a pre-existing test used the default path, so a machine holding `data/portal.db` passed where a CI runner could not. **`aaa41bb` fixed that at source on 2026-08-18 at 20:27:53**: `PricesCommand` builds its own database, and the commit message records the verification (*"data/ removed, key removed, as CI runs it: 576 passed, 2 skipped"*). The standing instruction issued alongside it — *"BEFORE EVERY PUSH: `data/` moved aside, `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` unset, full suite"* — was **re-issued 37 minutes later, at 21:04**, and then in every brief through 9c: measured at **seven occurrences across six units**, 2026-08-18 to 2026-08-21. It named no destination and no restore step. On 2026-08-20 at 21:19:50 a session moved the 137 MB corpus to a `/tmp` scratchpad, hit its usage limit at 21:53:52 with `You've hit your session limit`, and never moved it back; `/tmp` was cleared and **`data/portal.db` no longer exists anywhere**. The class this project had not named: **a mitigation is a liability with no owner and no expiry.** The defect it names is fixed and testable; the mitigation is prose, is copied forward by whoever writes the next brief, and is never re-derived against the thing it mitigates. It is M1.73's scalar with a destructive side effect — and unlike M1.73's counters, **nothing could have caught it, because it never entered the repository at all.** Six units of review passed over it because review reads the repository, and this lived only in prompts. **CI had been falsifying it on every run since Unit 5**: M1.65's runners have no `data/` to move, so every green run was a proof the instruction was unnecessary, and nobody read it as one. | **The instruction is dead and the property it wanted is a test.** *(a)* **The correct convention, stated positively so it cannot be re-derived as the old one: a session that must run without the corpus visible MOVES NOTHING.** It points the code under test at a temporary database — `tmp_path`, or a database the command builds itself, which is what `aaa41bb` made possible. Mutating the working tree to change a test result is the instruction's whole defect: it makes the developer's filesystem an input to the suite, which is the very coupling Unit 7 was fixing. *(b)* **`tests/conftest.py`** fails the session if `data/` was absent when it began and present when it ended — `db.connect` mkdirs the parent, so an empty file at `config.DEFAULT_DB_PATH` is enough to trip it. Negative control run before it was trusted: a test calling `db.connect(config.DEFAULT_DB_PATH)` **passes** while the **session fails**, which is the shape that catches a defect no individual assertion is about. *(c)* **What the guard cannot do is written into its docstring rather than left to be found**: on a machine that already holds `data/`, before and after are both *present* and it is silent — so, exactly as M1.64 and M1.19 already rule, the authority is the run that gates the merge. **Nothing was deleted from the repository, because a grep for the instruction over every tracked file returns nothing** — and that absence is the finding, not a clean bill of health. | §10.4b, and every future brief |
| M1.96 | **§10.4b carries the scar of the lost M5 stash and has never said what the corpus is.** The database was 137 MB, gitignored by `.gitignore:5-8`, present in no backup, and named as the evidence base for measurements in §10.2, §10.3 and eleven amendment rows — and no section of this spec stated whether losing it was an accident or an accepted cost. A resource with no stated durability is defended by whatever habit the current brief happens to carry, which is precisely how M1.95 happened. | **RULED: the corpus is DISPOSABLE, and the reason is that its irreplaceable input is already durable.** `seeds/candidates.csv` — 13 domains, platform verified by hand — **is committed**, and every finding derived from the corpus is committed prose. What was lost is *derived* data: fetched bodies and the signals computed from them, all of which `fetch` and `extract-p1` reproduce from the seed list at zero monetary cost. **Recovery procedure, stated explicitly so it is not re-derived under pressure:** `portal init` → `portal fetch --seed seeds/candidates.csv` → `portal extract-p1` → `portal score --phase 1`.
